I’ve tried to get there, if that makes sense. I’ve tried to say, “All right, maybe none of this is intentional, maybe I’m putting too much weight on it.” And there are moments when I can almost step outside it and see how someone else would explain it away.\n\nLike if it’s a schedule change, they can show an email. If it’s a seating decision, there’s some reason about balance or practicality. If it’s a comment in rehearsal, it gets framed as musical feedback. And I do understand that those explanations exist. I’m not saying there’s never paperwork or a reason attached.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve ever fully stopped believing it. That’s why I’m here, really. For almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using those kinds of things—schedule changes, seating choices, little comments in rehearsals—to quietly undermine my work. Even when there’s an email or a note that supposedly explains it, I still come back to the feeling that the explanation is covering something deliberate.\n\nSo no, I’ve had moments of trying to doubt it, but not a time where I completely believed it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any moments when your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members significantly weakens?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are moments when it weakens a little, but I don’t think it ever really goes away.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m away from the ensemble for a bit, or when I’m looking at one thing by itself. Like if someone shows me an email about a schedule change, I can sit there and think, “Okay, maybe this really was just availability.” Or with a seating decision, I can admit there might be a practical explanation. And with comments in rehearsal, sometimes I’ll think, “Maybe that was just musical feedback and I’m hearing it through everything that’s happened.”\n\nSo in those moments, yes, my certainty can loosen a bit. I can sort of talk myself down. But then when I look at the whole thing together, over almost two years, after the commission… it comes back. It’s not one isolated schedule change or one seating choice or one strange comment. It’s the accumulation of it, the way a few people seem to use these small, ordinary-looking things to shift how my work is received or to put me in a worse position.\n\nThat’s the part I can’t shake, even when there are emails or notes that supposedly explain it. I can see the explanation, but it doesn’t really make the pattern disappear for me. So yes, it weakens sometimes in the moment, but not enough that I stop believing it’s deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or events that make you more or less certain about being undermined by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It gets stronger when something happens that looks ordinary on the surface but fits the same pattern I’ve been seeing.\n\nFor example, a schedule change by itself might not mean much. I know that. Ensembles change schedules all the time. But when it affects when my piece is rehearsed, or who’s there, or how much time it gets, then I start thinking, “Here it is again.” And if someone shows me an email afterward saying it was because of availability, I can understand the explanation, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It still feels like the kind of explanation that makes it look normal from the outside.\n\nThe same thing happens with seating choices. If certain players are moved around, or I’m placed in a situation where I feel like I have less control over how the work is heard, that makes me more certain. Like, if I only look at one email or one rehearsal note, I can say, “Fine, maybe that one thing has an innocent explanation.” But once I put it back into the bigger picture—almost two years of schedule changes, seating choices, and those little rehearsal comments after the commission—I come right back to feeling sure that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instance where you felt undermined but later found out it was due to a misunderstanding or miscommunication?\n\nPatient: I can think of times where someone tried to explain it afterward as a misunderstanding, yes. Like with a schedule change, for example, they’ll show me an email chain and say, “No, this was just availability,” or, “This rehearsal slot moved because someone had a conflict.” And on paper, I can see how that looks like miscommunication or logistics.\n\nOr with seating, someone might say I misunderstood the reason—that it was about balance, or space, or some practical issue I wasn’t aware of. That’s what I can’t let go of.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings about being undermined by the ensemble changed in intensity over the nearly two years this has been happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has changed, but maybe not in a simple straight line. At first, after the commission, I was more confused than anything. I noticed things, but I kept trying to tell myself there were normal explanations—because they always had normal explanations. A schedule change would come with an email. A seating choice would be explained as practical. A comment in rehearsal would be framed like it was just musical feedback, nothing personal.\n\nBut over time, almost two years now, it’s become harder for me to dismiss it. The intensity has built because it keeps happening in these small ways, and the pattern feels too consistent. I’m here because I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately—using those little adjustments and comments to quietly undermine my work, while still making sure there’s always some note or message they can point to that makes it look innocent.\n\nSome days I can function around it better than others. I can tell myself, “Just focus on the work, don’t react.” But the conviction underneath hasn’t really gone away. If anything, the longer it goes on, the more convinced I feel that I’m seeing something real, even if I can’t prove it in a way that would satisfy anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried to consider that. I know that’s probably the reasonable thing to say, and honestly it’s what I kept telling myself at the beginning. Like, “Maybe I’m reading into this. Maybe the schedule change really was just logistical. Maybe the seating choice had nothing to do with me. Maybe that comment in rehearsal only sounded pointed because I was already tense.”\n\nAnd I do understand that when they show me an email or a rehearsal note or some practical explanation, it can look very ordinary from the outside. That’s part of what makes this so hard to talk about, because I hear myself explaining it and I know how it can sound.\n\nBut… I’m here because even after trying to doubt myself, I can’t shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things deliberately. Not dramatic things, not anything obvious. It’s more like little schedule adjustments, seating decisions, comments in rehearsal that shift how people hear my work or how I’m positioned. And every time, there’s some explanation ready, which supposedly makes it innocent.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered that I might be mistaken. I’ve gone over that possibility again and again. But after almost two years, it still doesn’t feel mistaken to me. It feels like a pattern I’m seeing clearly, even if I can’t prove it in a way that convinces someone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you doubted your belief that the ensemble members are undermining you?\n\nPatient: There have been moments where I’ve tried to doubt it, yes. Especially early on, I really did try to talk myself out of it. I’d think, “Maybe this is just how ensembles work, maybe I’m being sensitive because the commission mattered so much to me, maybe I’m connecting things that aren’t connected.”\n\nAnd even now, when someone shows me an email or points to a rehearsal note, I can see how it’s supposed to explain things. Like, “No, the schedule changed because of availability,” or “The seating was just about balance,” or “That comment was just ordinary feedback.” I understand that those explanations exist.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say the doubt lasts. That’s the problem. I can step back for a little while and try to be fair, but then I look at the whole pattern again—nearly two years of these small changes and comments after the commission—and I come back to the same certainty. It feels too consistent to be accidental.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had periods where I questioned myself, but I’ve never really been able to shake the belief that a few people in the ensemble are deliberately undermining my work in quiet ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you completely stopped believing that the ensemble members were undermining you?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. I’ve tried to get there, if that makes sense. I’ve tried to say, “All right, maybe none of this is intentional, maybe I’m putting too much weight on it.” And there are moments when I can almost step outside it and see how someone else would explain it away.\n\nLike if it’s a schedule change, they can show an email. If it’s a seating decision, there’s some reason about balance or practicality. If it’s a comment in rehearsal, it gets framed as musical feedback. And I do understand that those explanations exist. I’m not saying there’s never paperwork or a reason attached.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve ever fully stopped believing it. That’s why I’m here, really. For almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using those kinds of things—schedule changes, seating choices, little comments in rehearsals—to quietly undermine my work. Even when there’s an email or a note that supposedly explains it, I still come back to the feeling that the explanation is covering something deliberate.\n\nSo no, I’ve had moments of trying to doubt it, but not a time where I completely believed it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any moments when your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members significantly weakens?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are moments when it weakens a little, but I don’t think it ever really goes away.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m away from the ensemble for a bit, or when I’m looking at one thing by itself. Like if someone shows me an email about a schedule change, I can sit there and think, “Okay, maybe this really was just availability.” Or with a seating decision, I can admit there might be a practical explanation. And with comments in rehearsal, sometimes I’ll think, “Maybe that was just musical feedback and I’m hearing it through everything that’s happened.”\n\nSo in those moments, yes, my certainty can loosen a bit. I can sort of talk myself down. But then when I look at the whole thing together, over almost two years, after the commission… it comes back. It’s not one isolated schedule change or one seating choice or one strange comment. It’s the accumulation of it, the way a few people seem to use these small, ordinary-looking things to shift how my work is received or to put me in a worse position.\n\nThat’s the part I can’t shake, even when there are emails or notes that supposedly explain it. I can see the explanation, but it doesn’t really make the pattern disappear for me. So yes, it weakens sometimes in the moment, but not enough that I stop believing it’s deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or events that make you more or less certain about being undermined by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It gets stronger when something happens that looks ordinary on the surface but fits the same pattern I’ve been seeing.\n\nFor example, a schedule change by itself might not mean much. I know that. Ensembles change schedules all the time. But when it affects when my piece is rehearsed, or who’s there, or how much time it gets, then I start thinking, “Here it is again.” And if someone shows me an email afterward saying it was because of availability, I can understand the explanation, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It still feels like the kind of explanation that makes it look normal from the outside.\n\nThe same thing happens with seating choices. If certain players are moved around, or I’m placed in a situation where I feel like I have less control over how the work is heard, that makes me more certain. Again, I know there are musical reasons people give—balance, logistics, whatever—but after the commission, these little choices started feeling too consistent to just be practical decisions.\n\nAnd comments in rehearsal are a big one. Not open attacks, not anything dramatic. It’s more the small things—remarks that sound like feedback, but have this edge to them, or seem timed in a way that makes my work seem less serious or less prepared. That’s when I feel most certain, because it’s quiet enough that if I point it out, I look unreasonable, but it still changes how people in the room receive the piece.\n\nI’m less certain when I’m away from it, or if I force myself to look at just one incident in isolation. Like, if I only look at one email or one rehearsal note, I can say, “Fine, maybe that one thing has an innocent explanation.” But once I put it back into the bigger picture—almost two years of schedule changes, seating choices, and those little rehearsal comments after the commission—I come right back to feeling sure that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instance where you felt undermined but later found out it was due to a misunderstanding or miscommunication?\n\nPatient: I can think of times where someone tried to explain it afterward as a misunderstanding, yes. Like with a schedule change, for example, they’ll show me an email chain and say, “No, this was just availability,” or, “This rehearsal slot moved because someone had a conflict.” And on paper, I can see how that looks like miscommunication or logistics.\n\nOr with seating, someone might say I misunderstood the reason—that it was about balance, or space, or some practical issue I wasn’t aware of. And I’ll try to take that in. I really do try to sit with it and think, “Okay, maybe I read too much into that one.”\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve ever found out in a way that actually changed my belief about the overall thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. One incident can be explained, maybe. A note can be produced. An email can make something look ordinary. But over almost two years, after the commission, it’s the pattern that doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding to me.\n\nSo… if you mean, have there been individual moments where there was an explanation that sounded reasonable, yes. But if you mean did I later realize, “Oh, I was wrong, they weren’t undermining me,” no. I haven’t really been able to get there. Even when the explanation is there, it still feels like it may be covering something deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal or professional stressors or life changes outside of your work with the ensemble during the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything major that I can point to.\n\nI mean, life has had the usual pressures. Work can be demanding, and being in music, especially at this stage, there’s always some uncertainty and pressure to prove yourself. But if you mean some big life event—like a major loss, a breakup, a move that disrupted everything, or something outside the ensemble that suddenly changed my life—I can’t really think of anything like that over the past two years.\n\nMost of what has felt stressful has been connected to the ensemble situation itself, honestly. I know that may sound like I’m narrowing everything back to that, but that’s where the tension has been for me. Outside of that, things have been relatively normal. Busy, sometimes tiring, but not some separate crisis that explains why I started seeing things differently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’d like to say yes, because I don’t want to be someone who just refuses evidence. I mean, I know how that sounds. I know if someone says, “Nothing could ever change my mind,” that sounds… closed off, or unreasonable. And I don’t think of myself that way.

But honestly, it would have to be something very convincing, and I don’t really know what that would be at this point. Because for almost two years now, I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things in small, quiet ways—schedule changes, seating decisions, little comments in rehearsals—that affected how my work was heard and how people responded to it.

The difficult part is that they can always produce an explanation. They’ll show me an email or some note that supposedly explains why the rehearsal time changed, or why someone was seated differently, or why a comment was just ordinary feedback. And I can understand those explanations in the moment. I’m not saying I can’t read an email and see what it says. But it doesn’t really change the larger pattern for me. It feels like those explanations are part of why it’s so hard to point to directly—because each individual thing can be made to look normal.

So yes, in theory, new information could change how I think about a specific incident. Like, I might say, “Fine, that one rehearsal change really was just availability.” But changing the whole belief? That would be much harder. Because this isn’t based on one thing. It’s the accumulation of all these small things after the commission, and the way they keep lining up. That’s what I keep coming back to.

It still feels pointed.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that whenever I try to explain one incident, someone can pull out an email or say, “Oh, that was just logistics,” or “That’s where people happened to be seated,” or “You’re reading into it.” But it’s the pattern. It’s been almost two years of this, and I can’t shake the certainty that a few of them are deliberately trying to quietly undermine my work without making it obvious enough that I can prove it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like they are undermining you only in the context of your work, or is it happening in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly in the context of the ensemble and my work with them. That’s the thing—I don’t want to make it sound like my whole life has fallen apart or like I’m seeing this everywhere, because I’m not.\n\nOutside of that situation, I’m still functioning pretty normally, I think. I meet deadlines, I keep composing, I’m not missing obligations. I still run most mornings, which helps me clear my head, even if sometimes I end up thinking about all of this while I’m running. And at home, with my wife, things are basically steady. It’s not like this is spilling into every relationship or every part of my day in some obvious way.\n\nBut with the ensemble, and anything connected to that work, that’s where it feels concentrated. That’s where the pattern keeps showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever confronted any of these people directly about your feelings that they are undermining you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it a direct confrontation, exactly. I’ve come close. I’ve asked questions in the moment, like, “Why was that changed?” or “Was there a reason I wasn’t told earlier?” or I’ll press a little on a seating decision or a rehearsal comment, but I usually try to keep it sounding practical, because the second I say what I actually think is happening, I know how it will land.\n\nThere have been a couple of conversations where I probably sounded more tense than I meant to. I remember trying to explain that the timing of certain changes was affecting the work, and I could tell the other person was hearing it as me being difficult or oversensitive. And then they gave some logistical explanation, which is exactly the problem—it always has an explanation if you isolate it.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t sat someone down and said, “I think you’re deliberately undermining me.” Not in those words. I’ve wanted to. I’ve rehearsed it in my head more times than I’d like to admit. But I worry that if I say it outright, then they’ll just use that too, like, “See, he’s paranoid,” or “He can’t handle criticism,” or something like that. So I keep it more indirect, asking about specifics, trying to catch the inconsistency without making myself look unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever caught anyone in the act of undermining you, or is it always after the fact that you piece together the pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s usually after the fact, if I’m being completely honest. I mean, I’ve never walked in on someone saying, “Let’s make him look bad,” or seen some message where they admit it. If I had that, I probably wouldn’t be sitting here trying to explain why it’s real. It would just be obvious.\n\nWhat happens is more… it’s in the way things line up. A schedule change gets made that supposedly has some normal reason, and then it puts me in a weaker position. Or a seating choice is presented like it’s just practical, but it changes how the piece comes across. Or someone makes a small comment in rehearsal that sounds harmless if you repeat it later, but in that exact moment, with that exact group of people listening, it lands like a cue for everyone to doubt me.\n\nAnd then when I ask about it, there’s always something they can point to—an email, a note, some explanation that makes it sound ordinary. That’s the part that makes me feel almost trapped, because each thing by itself can be explained away. But after almost two years, after the commission, I can’t shake the certainty that a few of them are doing it deliberately, quietly, in ways that won’t look like anything if I try to prove it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular person in the ensemble who you feel is primarily responsible for undermining your work?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s one person, no. I know that would probably make it easier to explain—like if I could point to one conductor or one administrator and say, “That’s the person doing it.” But it hasn’t felt that simple.\n\nIt’s more like a few people in the ensemble, after the commission, started acting in ways that lined up too neatly. One person might be involved in a schedule change, another in a seating decision, another makes one of those small rehearsal comments that sounds innocent if you repeat it later, but in the room it changes the atmosphere. And then if I ask, they can show me an email or a note or some ordinary explanation, which makes it look like I’m overreacting.\n\nSo no, not one ringleader exactly. But I do feel certain there are a few of them who know what they’re doing. Quietly. Carefully. In a way that makes it almost impossible to prove without sounding unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you started to feel like people outside of the ensemble are also against you or plotting something?\n\nPatient: Not really outside the ensemble, no. I mean… I’ve worried about who they might be talking to, because in music circles people overlap and word gets around, but I don’t have the same feeling about, say, strangers or people unrelated to the work. It’s not like I think everyone is against me.\n\nThe thing that brought me here is much more specific. It’s been almost two years of feeling certain that after that commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these small things—schedule changes, seating choices, little rehearsal comments—that quietly put me in a worse position or made my work seem weaker. And when I ask about it, they can always show some email or rehearsal note or practical explanation, which makes it sound normal. But taken together, it doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it has spread broadly outside of that. It’s mainly centered on those people and that situation. That’s what I can’t let go of.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings about being undermined by the ensemble changed in intensity over the nearly two years this has been happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has changed, but maybe not in a simple straight line. At first, after the commission, I was more confused than anything. I noticed things, but I kept trying to tell myself there were normal explanations—because they always had normal explanations. A schedule change would come with an email. A seating choice would be explained as practical. A comment in rehearsal would be framed like it was just musical feedback, nothing personal.\n\nBut over time, almost two years now, it’s become harder for me to dismiss it. The intensity has built because it keeps happening in these small ways, and the pattern feels too consistent. I’m here because I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately—using those little adjustments and comments to quietly undermine my work, while still making sure there’s always some note or message they can point to that makes it look innocent.\n\nSome days I can function around it better than others. I can tell myself, “Just focus on the work, don’t react.” But the conviction underneath hasn’t really gone away. If anything, the longer it goes on, the more convinced I feel that I’m seeing something real, even if I can’t prove it in a way that would satisfy anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried to consider that. I know that’s probably the reasonable thing to say, and honestly it’s what I kept telling myself at the beginning. Like, “Maybe I’m reading into this. Maybe the schedule change really was just logistical. Maybe the seating choice had nothing to do with me. Maybe that comment in rehearsal only sounded pointed because I was already tense.”\n\nAnd I do understand that when they show me an email or a rehearsal note or some practical explanation, it can look very ordinary from the outside. That’s part of what makes this so hard to talk about, because I hear myself explaining it and I know how it can sound.\n\nBut… I’m here because even after trying to doubt myself, I can’t shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things deliberately. Not dramatic things, not anything obvious. It’s more like little schedule adjustments, seating decisions, comments in rehearsal that shift how people hear my work or how I’m positioned. And every time, there’s some explanation ready, which supposedly makes it innocent.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered that I might be mistaken. I’ve gone over that possibility again and again. But after almost two years, it still doesn’t feel mistaken to me. It feels like a pattern I’m seeing clearly, even if I can’t prove it in a way that convinces someone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you doubted your belief that the ensemble members are undermining you?\n\nPatient: There have been moments where I’ve tried to doubt it, yes. Especially early on, I really did try to talk myself out of it. I’d think, “Maybe this is just how ensembles work, maybe I’m being sensitive because the commission mattered so much to me, maybe I’m connecting things that aren’t connected.”\n\nAnd even now, when someone shows me an email or points to a rehearsal note, I can see how it’s supposed to explain things. Like, “No, the schedule changed because of availability,” or “The seating was just about balance,” or “That comment was just ordinary feedback.” I understand that those explanations exist.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say the doubt lasts. That’s the problem. I can step back for a little while and try to be fair, but then I look at the whole pattern again—nearly two years of these small changes and comments after the commission—and I come back to the same certainty. It feels too consistent to be accidental.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had periods where I questioned myself, but I’ve never really been able to shake the belief that a few people in the ensemble are deliberately undermining my work in quiet ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you completely stopped believing that the ensemble members were undermining you?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. I’ve tried to get there, if that makes sense. I’ve tried to say, “All right, maybe none of this is intentional, maybe I’m putting too much weight on it.” And there are moments when I can almost step outside it and see how someone else would explain it away.\n\nLike if it’s a schedule change, they can show an email. If it’s a seating decision, there’s some reason about balance or practicality. If it’s a comment in rehearsal, it gets framed as musical feedback. And I do understand that those explanations exist. I’m not saying there’s never paperwork or a reason attached.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve ever fully stopped believing it. That’s why I’m here, really. For almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using those kinds of things—schedule changes, seating choices, little comments in rehearsals—to quietly undermine my work. Even when there’s an email or a note that supposedly explains it, I still come back to the feeling that the explanation is covering something deliberate.\n\nSo no, I’ve had moments of trying to doubt it, but not a time where I completely believed it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any moments when your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members significantly weakens?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are moments when it weakens a little, but I don’t think it ever really goes away.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m away from the ensemble for a bit, or when I’m looking at one thing by itself. Like if someone shows me an email about a schedule change, I can sit there and think, “Okay, maybe this really was just availability.” Or with a seating decision, I can admit there might be a practical explanation. And with comments in rehearsal, sometimes I’ll think, “Maybe that was just musical feedback and I’m hearing it through everything that’s happened.”\n\nSo in those moments, yes, my certainty can loosen a bit. I can sort of talk myself down. But then when I look at the whole thing together, over almost two years, after the commission… it comes back. It’s not one isolated schedule change or one seating choice or one strange comment. It’s the accumulation of it, the way a few people seem to use these small, ordinary-looking things to shift how my work is received or to put me in a worse position.\n\nThat’s the part I can’t shake, even when there are emails or notes that supposedly explain it. I can see the explanation, but it doesn’t really make the pattern disappear for me. So yes, it weakens sometimes in the moment, but not enough that I stop believing it’s deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or events that make you more or less certain about being undermined by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It gets stronger when something happens that looks ordinary on the surface but fits the same pattern I’ve been seeing.\n\nFor example, a schedule change by itself might not mean much. I know that. Ensembles change schedules all the time. But when it affects when my piece is rehearsed, or who’s there, or how much time it gets, then I start thinking, “Here it is again.” And if someone shows me an email afterward saying it was because of availability, I can understand the explanation, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It still feels like the kind of explanation that makes it look normal from the outside.\n\nThe same thing happens with seating choices. If certain players are moved around, or I’m placed in a situation where I feel like I have less control over how the work is heard, that makes me more certain. Again, I know there are musical reasons people give—balance, logistics, whatever—but after the commission, these little choices started feeling too consistent to just be practical decisions.\n\nAnd comments in rehearsal are a big one. Not open attacks, not anything dramatic. It’s more the small things—remarks that sound like feedback, but have this edge to them, or seem timed in a way that makes my work seem less serious or less prepared. That’s when I feel most certain, because it’s quiet enough that if I point it out, I look unreasonable, but it still changes how people in the room receive the piece.\n\nI’m less certain when I’m away from it, or if I force myself to look at just one incident in isolation. Like, if I only look at one email or one rehearsal note, I can say, “Fine, maybe that one thing has an innocent explanation.” But once I put it back into the bigger picture—almost two years of schedule changes, seating choices, and those little rehearsal comments after the commission—I come right back to feeling sure that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instance where you felt undermined but later found out it was due to a misunderstanding or miscommunication?\n\nPatient: I can think of times where someone tried to explain it afterward as a misunderstanding, yes. Like with a schedule change, for example, they’ll show me an email chain and say, “No, this was just availability,” or, “This rehearsal slot moved because someone had a conflict.” And on paper, I can see how that looks like miscommunication or logistics.\n\nOr with seating, someone might say I misunderstood the reason—that it was about balance, or space, or some practical issue I wasn’t aware of. And I’ll try to take that in. I really do try to sit with it and think, “Okay, maybe I read too much into that one.”\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve ever found out in a way that actually changed my belief about the overall thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. One incident can be explained, maybe. A note can be produced. An email can make something look ordinary. But over almost two years, after the commission, it’s the pattern that doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding to me.\n\nSo… if you mean, have there been individual moments where there was an explanation that sounded reasonable, yes. But if you mean did I later realize, “Oh, I was wrong, they weren’t undermining me,” no. I haven’t really been able to get there. Even when the explanation is there, it still feels like it may be covering something deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal or professional stressors or life changes outside of your work with the ensemble during the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything major that I can point to.\n\nI mean, life has had the usual pressures. Work can be demanding, and being in music, especially at this stage, there’s always some uncertainty and pressure to prove yourself. But if you mean some big life event—like a major loss, a breakup, a move that disrupted everything, or something outside the ensemble that suddenly changed my life—I can’t really think of anything like that over the past two years.\n\nMost of what has felt stressful has been connected to the ensemble situation itself, honestly. I know that may sound like I’m narrowing everything back to that, but that’s where the tension has been for me. Outside of that, things have been relatively normal. Busy, sometimes tiring, but not some separate crisis that explains why I started seeing things differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about the ensemble members undermining you could change based on new information or evidence?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because I don’t want to be someone who just refuses evidence. I mean, I know how that sounds. I know if someone says, “Nothing could ever change my mind,” that sounds… closed off, or unreasonable. And I don’t think of myself that way.\n\nBut honestly, it would have to be something very convincing, and I don’t really know what that would be at this point. Because for almost two years now, I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things in small, quiet ways—schedule changes, seating decisions, little comments in rehearsals—that affected how my work was heard and how people responded to it.\n\nThe difficult part is that they can always produce an explanation. They’ll show me an email or some note that supposedly explains why the rehearsal time changed, or why someone was seated differently, or why a comment was just ordinary feedback. And I can understand those explanations in the moment. I’m not saying I can’t read an email and see what it says. But it doesn’t really change the larger pattern for me. It feels like those explanations are part of why it’s so hard to point to directly—because each individual thing can be made to look normal.\n\nSo yes, in theory, new information could change how I think about a specific incident. Like, I might say, “Fine, that one rehearsal change really was just availability.” But changing the whole belief? That would be much harder. Because this isn’t based on one thing. It’s the accumulation of all these small things after the commission, and the way they keep lining up. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ve tried to get there, if that makes sense. If it’s a seating decision, there’s some reason about balance or practicality. If it’s a comment in rehearsal, it gets framed as musical feedback. And I do understand that those explanations exist. I’m not saying there’s never paperwork or a reason attached.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve ever fully stopped believing it. That’s why I’m here, really. For almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using those kinds of things—schedule changes, seating choices, little comments in rehearsals—to quietly undermine my work. Even when there’s an email or a note that supposedly explains it, I still come back to the feeling that the explanation is covering something deliberate.\n\nSo no, I’ve had moments of trying to doubt it, but not a time where I completely believed it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any moments when your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members significantly weakens?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are moments when it weakens a little, but I don’t think it ever really goes away.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m away from the ensemble for a bit, or when I’m looking at one thing by itself. Like if someone shows me an email about a schedule change, I can sit there and think, “Okay, maybe this really was just availability.” Or with a seating decision, I can admit there might be a practical explanation. And with comments in rehearsal, sometimes I’ll think, “Maybe that was just musical feedback and I’m hearing it through everything that’s happened.”\n\nSo in those moments, yes, my certainty can loosen a bit. I can sort of talk myself down. But then when I look at the whole thing together, over almost two years, after the commission… it comes back. It’s not one isolated schedule change or one seating choice or one strange comment. It’s the accumulation of it, the way a few people seem to use these small, ordinary-looking things to shift how my work is received or to put me in a worse position.\n\nThat’s the part I can’t shake, even when there are emails or notes that supposedly explain it. I can see the explanation, but it doesn’t really make the pattern disappear for me. So yes, it weakens sometimes in the moment, but not enough that I stop believing it’s deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or events that make you more or less certain about being undermined by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It gets stronger when something happens that looks ordinary on the surface but fits the same pattern I’ve been seeing.\n\nFor example, a schedule change by itself might not mean much. I know that. Ensembles change schedules all the time. But when it affects when my piece is rehearsed, or who’s there, or how much time it gets, then I start thinking, “Here it is again.” And if someone shows me an email afterward saying it was because of availability, I can understand the explanation, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It still feels like the kind of explanation that makes it look normal from the outside.\n\nThe same thing happens with seating choices. If certain players are moved around, or I’m placed in a situation where I feel like I have less control over how the work is heard, that makes me more certain. Again, I know there are musical reasons people give—balance, logistics, whatever—but after the commission, these little choices started feeling too consistent to just be practical decisions.\n\nAnd comments in rehearsal are a big one. Not open attacks, not anything dramatic. It’s more the small things—remarks that sound like feedback, but have this edge to them, or seem timed in a way that makes my work seem less serious or less prepared. That’s when I feel most certain, because it’s quiet enough that if I point it out, I look unreasonable, but it still changes how people in the room receive the piece.\n\nI’m less certain when I’m away from it, or if I force myself to look at just one incident in isolation. Like, if I only look at one email or one rehearsal note, I can say, “Fine, maybe that one thing has an innocent explanation.” But once I put it back into the bigger picture—almost two years of schedule changes, seating choices, and those little rehearsal comments after the commission—I come right back to feeling sure that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instance where you felt undermined but later found out it was due to a misunderstanding or miscommunication?\n\nPatient: I can think of times where someone tried to explain it afterward as a misunderstanding, yes. Like with a schedule change, for example, they’ll show me an email chain and say, “No, this was just availability,” or, “This rehearsal slot moved because someone had a conflict.” And on paper, I can see how that looks like miscommunication or logistics.\n\nOr with seating, someone might say I misunderstood the reason—that it was about balance, or space, or some practical issue I wasn’t aware of. And I’ll try to take that in. I really do try to sit with it and think, “Okay, maybe I read too much into that one.”\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve ever found out in a way that actually changed my belief about the overall thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. One incident can be explained, maybe. A note can be produced. An email can make something look ordinary. But over almost two years, after the commission, it’s the pattern that doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding to me.\n\nSo… if you mean, have there been individual moments where there was an explanation that sounded reasonable, yes. But if you mean did I later realize, “Oh, I was wrong, they weren’t undermining me,” no. I haven’t really been able to get there. Even when the explanation is there, it still feels like it may be covering something deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal or professional stressors or life changes outside of your work with the ensemble during the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything major that I can point to.\n\nI mean, life has had the usual pressures. Work can be demanding, and being in music, especially at this stage, there’s always some uncertainty and pressure to prove yourself. But if you mean some big life event—like a major loss, a breakup, a move that disrupted everything, or something outside the ensemble that suddenly changed my life—I can’t really think of anything like that over the past two years.\n\nMost of what has felt stressful has been connected to the ensemble situation itself, honestly. I know that may sound like I’m narrowing everything back to that, but that’s where the tension has been for me. Outside of that, things have been relatively normal. Busy, sometimes tiring, but not some separate crisis that explains why I started seeing things differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about the ensemble members undermining you could change based on new information or evidence?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because I don’t want to be someone who just refuses evidence. I mean, I know how that sounds. I know if someone says, “Nothing could ever change my mind,” that sounds… closed off, or unreasonable. And I don’t think of myself that way.\n\nBut honestly, it would have to be something very convincing, and I don’t really know what that would be at this point. Because for almost two years now, I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things in small, quiet ways—schedule changes, seating decisions, little comments in rehearsals—that affected how my work was heard and how people responded to it.\n\nThe difficult part is that they can always produce an explanation. They’ll show me an email or some note that supposedly explains why the rehearsal time changed, or why someone was seated differently, or why a comment was just ordinary feedback. And I can understand those explanations in the moment. I’m not saying I can’t read an email and see what it says. But it doesn’t really change the larger pattern for me. It feels like those explanations are part of why it’s so hard to point to directly—because each individual thing can be made to look normal.\n\nSo yes, in theory, new information could change how I think about a specific incident. Like, I might say, “Fine, that one rehearsal change really was just availability.” But changing the whole belief? That would be much harder. Because this isn’t based on one thing. It’s the accumulation of all these small things after the commission, and the way they keep lining up. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really, no — at least not in a major way. I mean, I’ve probably been more preoccupied than I used to be, and I’m sure there are times when friends or family can tell my mind is on the ensemble situation. But outside of that, my relationships haven’t fallen apart or anything like that.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I’m here because this has been going on for almost two years now, and I can’t really shake it. After this commission I did, things started to feel… coordinated, I guess is the word. Like a few people in the ensemble began using schedule changes, where people were seated, little comments in rehearsals—small things that sound harmless if you repeat them back—to quietly undermine my work.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds, because if I bring up one specific thing, someone can show me an email or a note or some explanation that’s supposed to make it seem normal. But the pattern is what I can’t ignore. It’s not just one rehearsal being moved, or one seating decision, or one comment. It’s the way they line up, the timing of it, especially after the commission. I’ve tried to talk myself out of it, honestly, but I keep coming back to the same conclusion: they’re doing it deliberately, just subtly enough that I can’t prove it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people are plotting against you specifically?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I know “plotting” sounds dramatic, like I’m saying there’s some huge conspiracy with meetings and a written plan, and I’m not saying I’ve seen anything like that. But specifically against me, yes, I do think that.\n\nIt started after the commission, and before that things were not perfect, but they were normal enough. Then suddenly there were these schedule changes that would put me at a disadvantage, seating choices that affected how my work came across, and these little comments in rehearsals that, if you wrote them down, would look totally harmless. But in the room, with the timing, with who said what and when, it felt pointed. It still feels pointed.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that whenever I try to explain one incident, someone can pull out an email or say, “Oh, that was just logistics,” or “That’s where people happened to be seated,” or “You’re reading into it.” But it’s the pattern. It’s been almost two years of this, and I can’t shake the certainty that a few of them are deliberately trying to quietly undermine my work without making it obvious enough that I can prove it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like they are undermining you only in the context of your work, or is it happening in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly in the context of the ensemble and my work with them. That’s the thing—I don’t want to make it sound like my whole life has fallen apart or like I’m seeing this everywhere, because I’m not.\n\nOutside of that situation, I’m still functioning pretty normally, I think. I meet deadlines, I keep composing, I’m not missing obligations. I still run most mornings, which helps me clear my head, even if sometimes I end up thinking about all of this while I’m running. And at home, with my wife, things are basically steady. It’s not like this is spilling into every relationship or every part of my day in some obvious way.\n\nBut with the ensemble, and anything connected to that work, that’s where it feels concentrated. That’s where the pattern keeps showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever confronted any of these people directly about your feelings that they are undermining you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it a direct confrontation, exactly. I’ve come close. I’ve asked questions in the moment, like, “Why was that changed?” or “Was there a reason I wasn’t told earlier?” or I’ll press a little on a seating decision or a rehearsal comment, but I usually try to keep it sounding practical, because the second I say what I actually think is happening, I know how it will land.\n\nThere have been a couple of conversations where I probably sounded more tense than I meant to. I remember trying to explain that the timing of certain changes was affecting the work, and I could tell the other person was hearing it as me being difficult or oversensitive. And then they gave some logistical explanation, which is exactly the problem—it always has an explanation if you isolate it.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t sat someone down and said, “I think you’re deliberately undermining me.” Not in those words. I’ve wanted to. I’ve rehearsed it in my head more times than I’d like to admit. But I worry that if I say it outright, then they’ll just use that too, like, “See, he’s paranoid,” or “He can’t handle criticism,” or something like that. So I keep it more indirect, asking about specifics, trying to catch the inconsistency without making myself look unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever caught anyone in the act of undermining you, or is it always after the fact that you piece together the pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s usually after the fact, if I’m being completely honest. I mean, I’ve never walked in on someone saying, “Let’s make him look bad,” or seen some message where they admit it. If I had that, I probably wouldn’t be sitting here trying to explain why it’s real. It would just be obvious.\n\nWhat happens is more… it’s in the way things line up. A schedule change gets made that supposedly has some normal reason, and then it puts me in a weaker position. Or a seating choice is presented like it’s just practical, but it changes how the piece comes across. Or someone makes a small comment in rehearsal that sounds harmless if you repeat it later, but in that exact moment, with that exact group of people listening, it lands like a cue for everyone to doubt me.\n\nAnd then when I ask about it, there’s always something they can point to—an email, a note, some explanation that makes it sound ordinary. That’s the part that makes me feel almost trapped, because each thing by itself can be explained away. But after almost two years, after the commission, I can’t shake the certainty that a few of them are doing it deliberately, quietly, in ways that won’t look like anything if I try to prove it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular person in the ensemble who you feel is primarily responsible for undermining your work?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s one person, no. I know that would probably make it easier to explain—like if I could point to one conductor or one administrator and say, “That’s the person doing it.” But it hasn’t felt that simple.\n\nIt’s more like a few people in the ensemble, after the commission, started acting in ways that lined up too neatly. One person might be involved in a schedule change, another in a seating decision, another makes one of those small rehearsal comments that sounds innocent if you repeat it later, but in the room it changes the atmosphere. And then if I ask, they can show me an email or a note or some ordinary explanation, which makes it look like I’m overreacting.\n\nSo no, not one ringleader exactly. But I do feel certain there are a few of them who know what they’re doing. Quietly. Carefully. In a way that makes it almost impossible to prove without sounding unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you started to feel like people outside of the ensemble are also against you or plotting something?\n\nPatient: Not really outside the ensemble, no. I mean… I’ve worried about who they might be talking to, because in music circles people overlap and word gets around, but I don’t have the same feeling about, say, strangers or people unrelated to the work. It’s not like I think everyone is against me.\n\nThe thing that brought me here is much more specific. It’s been almost two years of feeling certain that after that commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these small things—schedule changes, seating choices, little rehearsal comments—that quietly put me in a worse position or made my work seem weaker. And when I ask about it, they can always show some email or rehearsal note or practical explanation, which makes it sound normal. But taken together, it doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it has spread broadly outside of that. It’s mainly centered on those people and that situation. That’s what I can’t let go of.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings about being undermined by the ensemble changed in intensity over the nearly two years this has been happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has changed, but maybe not in a simple straight line. At first, after the commission, I was more confused than anything. I noticed things, but I kept trying to tell myself there were normal explanations—because they always had normal explanations. A schedule change would come with an email. A seating choice would be explained as practical. A comment in rehearsal would be framed like it was just musical feedback, nothing personal.\n\nBut over time, almost two years now, it’s become harder for me to dismiss it. The intensity has built because it keeps happening in these small ways, and the pattern feels too consistent. I’m here because I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately—using those little adjustments and comments to quietly undermine my work, while still making sure there’s always some note or message they can point to that makes it look innocent.\n\nSome days I can function around it better than others. I can tell myself, “Just focus on the work, don’t react.” But the conviction underneath hasn’t really gone away. If anything, the longer it goes on, the more convinced I feel that I’m seeing something real, even if I can’t prove it in a way that would satisfy anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried to consider that. I know that’s probably the reasonable thing to say, and honestly it’s what I kept telling myself at the beginning. Like, “Maybe I’m reading into this. Maybe the schedule change really was just logistical. Maybe the seating choice had nothing to do with me. Maybe that comment in rehearsal only sounded pointed because I was already tense.”\n\nAnd I do understand that when they show me an email or a rehearsal note or some practical explanation, it can look very ordinary from the outside. That’s part of what makes this so hard to talk about, because I hear myself explaining it and I know how it can sound.\n\nBut… I’m here because even after trying to doubt myself, I can’t shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things deliberately. Not dramatic things, not anything obvious. It’s more like little schedule adjustments, seating decisions, comments in rehearsal that shift how people hear my work or how I’m positioned. And every time, there’s some explanation ready, which supposedly makes it innocent.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered that I might be mistaken. I’ve gone over that possibility again and again. But after almost two years, it still doesn’t feel mistaken to me. It feels like a pattern I’m seeing clearly, even if I can’t prove it in a way that convinces someone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you doubted your belief that the ensemble members are undermining you?\n\nPatient: There have been moments where I’ve tried to doubt it, yes. Especially early on, I really did try to talk myself out of it. I’d think, “Maybe this is just how ensembles work, maybe I’m being sensitive because the commission mattered so much to me, maybe I’m connecting things that aren’t connected.”\n\nAnd even now, when someone shows me an email or points to a rehearsal note, I can see how it’s supposed to explain things. Like, “No, the schedule changed because of availability,” or “The seating was just about balance,” or “That comment was just ordinary feedback.” I understand that those explanations exist.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say the doubt lasts. That’s the problem. I can step back for a little while and try to be fair, but then I look at the whole pattern again—nearly two years of these small changes and comments after the commission—and I come back to the same certainty. It feels too consistent to be accidental.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had periods where I questioned myself, but I’ve never really been able to shake the belief that a few people in the ensemble are deliberately undermining my work in quiet ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you completely stopped believing that the ensemble members were undermining you?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. I’ve tried to get there, if that makes sense. I’ve tried to say, “All right, maybe none of this is intentional, maybe I’m putting too much weight on it.” And there are moments when I can almost step outside it and see how someone else would explain it away.\n\nLike if it’s a schedule change, they can show an email. If it’s a seating decision, there’s some reason about balance or practicality. If it’s a comment in rehearsal, it gets framed as musical feedback. And I do understand that those explanations exist. I’m not saying there’s never paperwork or a reason attached.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve ever fully stopped believing it. That’s why I’m here, really. For almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using those kinds of things—schedule changes, seating choices, little comments in rehearsals—to quietly undermine my work. Even when there’s an email or a note that supposedly explains it, I still come back to the feeling that the explanation is covering something deliberate.\n\nSo no, I’ve had moments of trying to doubt it, but not a time where I completely believed it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any moments when your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members significantly weakens?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are moments when it weakens a little, but I don’t think it ever really goes away.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m away from the ensemble for a bit, or when I’m looking at one thing by itself. Like if someone shows me an email about a schedule change, I can sit there and think, “Okay, maybe this really was just availability.” Or with a seating decision, I can admit there might be a practical explanation. And with comments in rehearsal, sometimes I’ll think, “Maybe that was just musical feedback and I’m hearing it through everything that’s happened.”\n\nSo in those moments, yes, my certainty can loosen a bit. I can sort of talk myself down. But then when I look at the whole thing together, over almost two years, after the commission… it comes back. It’s not one isolated schedule change or one seating choice or one strange comment. It’s the accumulation of it, the way a few people seem to use these small, ordinary-looking things to shift how my work is received or to put me in a worse position.\n\nThat’s the part I can’t shake, even when there are emails or notes that supposedly explain it. I can see the explanation, but it doesn’t really make the pattern disappear for me. So yes, it weakens sometimes in the moment, but not enough that I stop believing it’s deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or events that make you more or less certain about being undermined by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It gets stronger when something happens that looks ordinary on the surface but fits the same pattern I’ve been seeing.\n\nFor example, a schedule change by itself might not mean much. I know that. Ensembles change schedules all the time. But when it affects when my piece is rehearsed, or who’s there, or how much time it gets, then I start thinking, “Here it is again.” And if someone shows me an email afterward saying it was because of availability, I can understand the explanation, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It still feels like the kind of explanation that makes it look normal from the outside.\n\nThe same thing happens with seating choices. If certain players are moved around, or I’m placed in a situation where I feel like I have less control over how the work is heard, that makes me more certain. Again, I know there are musical reasons people give—balance, logistics, whatever—but after the commission, these little choices started feeling too consistent to just be practical decisions.\n\nAnd comments in rehearsal are a big one. Not open attacks, not anything dramatic. It’s more the small things—remarks that sound like feedback, but have this edge to them, or seem timed in a way that makes my work seem less serious or less prepared. That’s when I feel most certain, because it’s quiet enough that if I point it out, I look unreasonable, but it still changes how people in the room receive the piece.\n\nI’m less certain when I’m away from it, or if I force myself to look at just one incident in isolation. Like, if I only look at one email or one rehearsal note, I can say, “Fine, maybe that one thing has an innocent explanation.” But once I put it back into the bigger picture—almost two years of schedule changes, seating choices, and those little rehearsal comments after the commission—I come right back to feeling sure that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instance where you felt undermined but later found out it was due to a misunderstanding or miscommunication?\n\nPatient: I can think of times where someone tried to explain it afterward as a misunderstanding, yes. Like with a schedule change, for example, they’ll show me an email chain and say, “No, this was just availability,” or, “This rehearsal slot moved because someone had a conflict.” And on paper, I can see how that looks like miscommunication or logistics.\n\nOr with seating, someone might say I misunderstood the reason—that it was about balance, or space, or some practical issue I wasn’t aware of. And I’ll try to take that in. I really do try to sit with it and think, “Okay, maybe I read too much into that one.”\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve ever found out in a way that actually changed my belief about the overall thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. One incident can be explained, maybe. A note can be produced. An email can make something look ordinary. But over almost two years, after the commission, it’s the pattern that doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding to me.\n\nSo… if you mean, have there been individual moments where there was an explanation that sounded reasonable, yes. But if you mean did I later realize, “Oh, I was wrong, they weren’t undermining me,” no. I haven’t really been able to get there. Even when the explanation is there, it still feels like it may be covering something deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal or professional stressors or life changes outside of your work with the ensemble during the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything major that I can point to.\n\nI mean, life has had the usual pressures. Work can be demanding, and being in music, especially at this stage, there’s always some uncertainty and pressure to prove yourself. But if you mean some big life event—like a major loss, a breakup, a move that disrupted everything, or something outside the ensemble that suddenly changed my life—I can’t really think of anything like that over the past two years.\n\nMost of what has felt stressful has been connected to the ensemble situation itself, honestly. I know that may sound like I’m narrowing everything back to that, but that’s where the tension has been for me. Outside of that, things have been relatively normal. Busy, sometimes tiring, but not some separate crisis that explains why I started seeing things differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about the ensemble members undermining you could change based on new information or evidence?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because I don’t want to be someone who just refuses evidence. I mean, I know how that sounds. I know if someone says, “Nothing could ever change my mind,” that sounds… closed off, or unreasonable. And I don’t think of myself that way.\n\nBut honestly, it would have to be something very convincing, and I don’t really know what that would be at this point. Because for almost two years now, I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things in small, quiet ways—schedule changes, seating decisions, little comments in rehearsals—that affected how my work was heard and how people responded to it.\n\nThe difficult part is that they can always produce an explanation. They’ll show me an email or some note that supposedly explains why the rehearsal time changed, or why someone was seated differently, or why a comment was just ordinary feedback. And I can understand those explanations in the moment. I’m not saying I can’t read an email and see what it says. But it doesn’t really change the larger pattern for me. It feels like those explanations are part of why it’s so hard to point to directly—because each individual thing can be made to look normal.\n\nSo yes, in theory, new information could change how I think about a specific incident. Like, I might say, “Fine, that one rehearsal change really was just availability.” But changing the whole belief? That would be much harder. Because this isn’t based on one thing. It’s the accumulation of all these small things after the commission, and the way they keep lining up. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a major way. I mean, I’ve probably been more preoccupied than I used to be, and I’m sure there are times when friends or family can tell my mind is on the ensemble situation. But outside of that, my relationships haven’t fallen apart or anything like that.\n\nThings at home with my wife are basically steady. We have normal stresses, normal conversations, normal disagreements sometimes, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some big change between us. She knows this has been weighing on me, obviously, because it’s hard not to talk about it when it affects my work and how I think about rehearsals and commissions and all of that. But our home life is still there. We still do the usual things.\n\nAnd with friends and family, I still keep in touch. Maybe I’m a little less casual than I used to be, or I don’t always feel like explaining everything because it can get exhausting. But I’m not isolated from everyone. I still meet deadlines, I’m still composing, I still run most mornings — that’s actually been one of the things that keeps me feeling somewhat level. So, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a dramatic change in my relationships outside of this specific issue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Ensembles change schedules all the time. But when it affects when my piece is rehearsed, or who’s there, or how much time it gets, then I start thinking, “Here it is again.” And if someone shows me an email afterward saying it was because of availability, I can understand the explanation, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It still feels like the kind of explanation that makes it look normal from the outside.\n\nThe same thing happens with seating choices. If certain players are moved around, or I’m placed in a situation where I feel like I have less control over how the work is heard, that makes me more certain. Again, I know there are musical reasons people give—balance, logistics, whatever—but after the commission, these little choices started feeling too consistent to just be practical decisions.\n\nAnd comments in rehearsal are a big one. Not open attacks, not anything dramatic. It’s more the small things—remarks that sound like feedback, but have this edge to them, or seem timed in a way that makes my work seem less serious or less prepared. That’s when I feel most certain, because it’s quiet enough that if I point it out, I look unreasonable, but it still changes how people in the room receive the piece.\n\nI’m less certain when I’m away from it, or if I force myself to look at just one incident in isolation. Like, if I only look at one email or one rehearsal note, I can say, “Fine, maybe that one thing has an innocent explanation.” But once I put it back into the bigger picture—almost two years of schedule changes, seating choices, and those little rehearsal comments after the commission—I come right back to feeling sure that a few people in the ensemble are doing it deliberately.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instance where you felt undermined but later found out it was due to a misunderstanding or miscommunication?\n\nPatient: I can think of times where someone tried to explain it afterward as a misunderstanding, yes. Like with a schedule change, for example, they’ll show me an email chain and say, “No, this was just availability,” or, “This rehearsal slot moved because someone had a conflict.” And on paper, I can see how that looks like miscommunication or logistics.\n\nOr with seating, someone might say I misunderstood the reason—that it was about balance, or space, or some practical issue I wasn’t aware of. And I’ll try to take that in. I really do try to sit with it and think, “Okay, maybe I read too much into that one.”\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve ever found out in a way that actually changed my belief about the overall thing. That’s the part I keep coming back to. One incident can be explained, maybe. A note can be produced. An email can make something look ordinary. But over almost two years, after the commission, it’s the pattern that doesn’t feel like a misunderstanding to me.\n\nSo… if you mean, have there been individual moments where there was an explanation that sounded reasonable, yes. But if you mean did I later realize, “Oh, I was wrong, they weren’t undermining me,” no. I haven’t really been able to get there. Even when the explanation is there, it still feels like it may be covering something deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal or professional stressors or life changes outside of your work with the ensemble during the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything major that I can point to.\n\nI mean, life has had the usual pressures. Work can be demanding, and being in music, especially at this stage, there’s always some uncertainty and pressure to prove yourself. But if you mean some big life event—like a major loss, a breakup, a move that disrupted everything, or something outside the ensemble that suddenly changed my life—I can’t really think of anything like that over the past two years.\n\nMost of what has felt stressful has been connected to the ensemble situation itself, honestly. I know that may sound like I’m narrowing everything back to that, but that’s where the tension has been for me. Outside of that, things have been relatively normal. Busy, sometimes tiring, but not some separate crisis that explains why I started seeing things differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about the ensemble members undermining you could change based on new information or evidence?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because I don’t want to be someone who just refuses evidence. I mean, I know how that sounds. I know if someone says, “Nothing could ever change my mind,” that sounds… closed off, or unreasonable. And I don’t think of myself that way.\n\nBut honestly, it would have to be something very convincing, and I don’t really know what that would be at this point. Because for almost two years now, I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things in small, quiet ways—schedule changes, seating decisions, little comments in rehearsals—that affected how my work was heard and how people responded to it.\n\nThe difficult part is that they can always produce an explanation. They’ll show me an email or some note that supposedly explains why the rehearsal time changed, or why someone was seated differently, or why a comment was just ordinary feedback. And I can understand those explanations in the moment. I’m not saying I can’t read an email and see what it says. But it doesn’t really change the larger pattern for me. It feels like those explanations are part of why it’s so hard to point to directly—because each individual thing can be made to look normal.\n\nSo yes, in theory, new information could change how I think about a specific incident. Like, I might say, “Fine, that one rehearsal change really was just availability.” But changing the whole belief? That would be much harder. Because this isn’t based on one thing. It’s the accumulation of all these small things after the commission, and the way they keep lining up. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a major way. I mean, I’ve probably been more preoccupied than I used to be, and I’m sure there are times when friends or family can tell my mind is on the ensemble situation. But outside of that, my relationships haven’t fallen apart or anything like that.\n\nThings at home with my wife are basically steady. We have normal stresses, normal conversations, normal disagreements sometimes, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some big change between us. She knows this has been weighing on me, obviously, because it’s hard not to talk about it when it affects my work and how I think about rehearsals and commissions and all of that. But our home life is still there. We still do the usual things.\n\nAnd with friends and family, I still keep in touch. Maybe I’m a little less casual than I used to be, or I don’t always feel like explaining everything because it can get exhausting. But I’m not isolated from everyone. I still meet deadlines, I’m still composing, I still run most mornings — that’s actually been one of the things that keeps me feeling somewhat level. So, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a dramatic change in my relationships outside of this specific issue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely? I… honestly, I don’t know. I want to say that if they could really show me something solid, something that accounted for the whole pattern and not just one isolated thing, then maybe. But that’s the problem — they usually can explain each piece in a way that sounds reasonable on its own.

Like, if I say the rehearsal schedule shifted in a way that hurt how my work came across, they can show an email about availability. If I bring up a seating choice that changed the balance, there’s some practical reason for it. If I mention a comment in rehearsal that felt pointed, they’ll say it was just ordinary feedback. And I can understand those explanations. I’m not saying I’m incapable of hearing them.

But for almost two years, I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these things quietly — not in some obvious, dramatic way, but through small decisions that affected how my work was heard and received. So when they give explanations, it doesn’t really erase that for me. It just feels like each individual thing gets made to look normal, while the larger pattern stays there.

So… could evidence change my mind completely? Maybe in theory. But in practice, I have a hard time imagining what would be strong enough, because the issue isn’t one email or one rehearsal note. It’s all of it together, and the timing, and the way it’s kept happening.
